=== BUGGY CODE ===
class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True
        
        def isForbidden(s):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False
        
        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i - truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res

Observe the following faulty python code. It contains one or more bugs.
Fix the code and output only the fixed code.

```python
class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True
        
        def isForbidden(s):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False
        
        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i - truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- staying in diagnosis mode for too long without testing the next plausible edit;
- moving from a local repair into a fresh branch of speculative causes when the current path has not been rechecked yet;
- turning a focused fix into a broad rewrite unless the surrounding code clearly forces it.

Original code:
```
class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True
        
        def isForbidden(s):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False
        
        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i - truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

However if a forbidden word is exactly "end", then t["end"]=True. But if another forbidden word starts with "e", then t["e"]={} and t["end"]=True both keys. isForbidden iterates characters, so it will never look up "end" as a character. There is a known problem "Longest Valid Substring" maybe LeetCode 2781? Let's recall. Problem: "Length of the Longest Valid Substring" (LeetCode 2781). Given word and forbidden, return length of longest valid substring (no forbidden string as substring). Constraints? I remember solution uses sliding window and trie. Common approach: For each right pointer, move left pointer to max(left, last_bad_start+1)? Actually there is a known solution:
```
class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        forbidden = set(forbidden)
        n = len(word)
        ans = 0
        left = 0
        for right in range(n):
            for i in range(right, max(right-10, left-1), -1):
                if word[i:right+1] in forbidden:
                    left = i + 1
                    break
            ans = max(ans, right-left+1)
        return ans
```
Because forbidden length <= 10. But the provided code uses trie and reverse scanning. Need fix to original intended? Let's search memory. LeetCode 2781 "Length of the Longest Valid Substring" has constraints: forbidden.length <= 1e5, forbidden[i].length <= 10, word.length <= 1e5. There is a known two-pointer solution with set and checking last 10 chars. But the given code is a different backward trie algorithm. Need ensure fixed code passes.

Let's analyze the given algorithm more deeply. It scans i from n-1 down to 0, maintains j. It checks isForbidden(word[i:j]) (forbidden prefix from i). If found, j = i - truc - 1 (bug). Maybe intended j = i + truc? Let's see if original algorithm might be different: Maybe they want to find longest substring ending at j? Let's derive from code's variable names.

`j = len(word)` initially. For i from n-1 downto 0:
- `truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])`
- if truc: `j = i - truc - 1`

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(s):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

This is minimal fix. But let's think if there is any hidden bug with `word[i:j]` when j is updated to i+truc-1. Suppose truc=1, j=i. Then for next i-1, word[i-1:i] is one char. Good. But what about res at current i: j-i = 0. Good. If j can equal i, then `word[i:j]` empty. isForbidden returns False. Good.

Now, if we want to avoid slicing and improve performance, we can write:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def forbidden_prefix_length(start: int, end: int) -> int:
            t = trie
            for pos in range(start, end):
                c = word[pos]
                if c not in t:
                    return 0
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return pos - start + 1
            return 0

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            L = forbidden_prefix_length(i, j)
            if L:
                j = i + L - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

This avoids slicing. But it changes `isForbidden` return False to 0. `if L:` works. Is this still "fixed code"? Yes. But is it too broad a rewrite? It's a local optimization. We should not over-optimize. However, the final code with slicing is correct but may be slow. The instruction "output only fixed code" doesn't ask for explanation. I think minimal fix is acceptable.

Maybe final code can be:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(s):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```
This is the most direct fix. If I output optimized index-based, it might be seen as more than necessary but still correct. The instruction "Output only the fixed code" doesn't say "minimal diff". Let's write final code:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        max_len = 0
        for f in forbidden:
            max_len = max(max_len, len(f))
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(s: str):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            end = min(j, i + max_len)
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:end])
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

Wait, if `forbidden` is empty, `max_len=0`, `end = min(j, i)`. If `j` is len(word), for i from n-1 downto 0, end=i, slice empty, isForbidden returns False, res = max(res, j-i) = n. Good. But note `j` remains n, so res = n. Good.

If `forbidden` contains empty string, max_len=0, but empty forbidden should invalidate all substrings? Original code wouldn't detect. Problem likely no empty. If we wanted to handle empty, we'd need special case. Not necessary.

Potential issue: If `max_len` is 0 and `forbidden` empty, `isForbidden("")` returns False. Good.

Potential issue: If `forbidden` contains strings but max_len computed. Good.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def forbidden_prefix_length(start: int, end: int) -> int:
            t = trie
            for pos in range(start, end):
                c = word[pos]
                if c not in t:
                    return 0
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return pos - start + 1
            return 0

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            L = forbidden_prefix_length(i, j)
            if L:
                j = i + L - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

This is correct and efficient. But note: If `forbidden` is empty, trie empty, `forbidden_prefix_length` returns0 for any range, j remains n, res=n. Good.

It only returns if it saw L chars, so L <= end-start = j-i. Thus i+L-1 <= j-1. So no min needed. But using min is harmless. If we use min, and due to some bug L > j-i, it would keep j smaller? Actually if L > j-i, i+L-1 > j-1, min(j, i+L-1) = j, no update, which would be wrong if a forbidden string longer than current window? But impossible. I'd use `j = i + L - 1` to mirror original fix. Or `j = min(j, i + L - 1)` for safety. Let's use `j = i + L - 1` because it's the direct fix. If someone worries about j increasing, it doesn't. But to be robust, `min` doesn't hurt. Let's test with a hypothetical if L=0? no update. If L=1, j=i. If L=j-i, j=i+j-i-1=j-1. Good.

Let's write final with `isForbidden(start, end)`:
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(start: int, end: int) -> int:
            t = trie
            for pos in range(start, end):
                c = word[pos]
                if c not in t:
                    return 0
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return pos - start + 1
            return 0

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```
This is good.

Now, let's think if the type hint `-> int` on nested function is okay. Yes.

Potential issue: If `j` is updated to `i + truc - 1`, and `truc` is an int, okay. If `truc` is 0, false. Good.

Now, let's consider if the original code's `isForbidden` returned `False` and the main code used `if truc:`. In Python, `False == 0`, so returning 0 is equivalent. Good.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with using `range(start, end)` when `end` is a Python int and `start` maybe negative? i never negative. Good.

As discussed, if such later interval exists, before processing i, j would already be <= that end-1, so `isForbidden(i, j)` wouldn't see the forbidden at i if its end is after j. If the forbidden at i's end is before the later interval, update. Good.

Let's test a scenario where a later forbidden interval has end smaller but `j` is not yet updated because that later interval starts at i+2 and we haven't processed i+2? Wait we scan from right to left. When processing i, we have already processed all positions > i, including i+2. So j already accounts for later intervals. Good.

Now, let's think if the helper should be called with `j` or `min(j, i+max_len)`? Since trie depth max_len, if `j` is huge, range(i,j) could be huge if word has a long path matching trie. But trie depth is max_len, so after max_len chars, either end or mismatch. However, the loop doesn't know max_len and will continue to `end` if every char is in t and no end. Can that happen beyond max_len? Suppose trie has a path of length max_len, and at the node at depth max_len, there is no "end" but there are children? There cannot be children beyond max_len because no forbidden string longer than max_len. But the node at depth max_len might have "end" if a forbidden string of length max_len exists. If it has "end", the loop would have returned at that position. If it doesn't have "end", then no children either (unless there is a forbidden string longer than max_len, impossible). If d == max_len, t may have "end" (if a forbidden string exactly length max_len) but no children. If no "end", then t is an empty dict? We followed a path of length max_len that is a prefix of some forbidden string of length > max_len? impossible. So depth max_len only reached if there is a forbidden string of length max_len, and then "end" exists, so return. Thus loop length <= max_len. Let's write final code carefully:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(start: int, end: int) -> int:
            t = trie
            for pos in range(start, end):
                c = word[pos]
                if c not in t:
                    return 0
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return pos - start + 1
            return 0

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

=== RAW ANSWER ===
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(start: int, end: int) -> int:
            t = trie
            for pos in range(start, end):
                c = word[pos]
                if c not in t:
                    return 0
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return pos - start + 1
            return 0

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res

=== FIXED CODE ===
